# Cross-Cutting Literature Synthesis (`lit_cross_cutting.md`)

**Dissertation Project:** Assessing the Environmental and Grid Sustainability of India's AI Data Center Expansion: A Policy and Quantitative Energy Modeling Analysis
**Researcher:** Chetan (Roll No. M2023BSASS013), B.S. Analytics and Sustainability Studies, Tata Institute of Social Sciences (TISS)
**Scope:** Systematic synthesis of cross-study themes, normalised metrics, definitions, multi-paper patterns, contradictions/gaps, methodological quality, empirical case studies, India-specific evidence, and direct indicator linkages to the dissertation master dataset.

---

# Part 1: Water Literature Synthesis (Cross-Cutting Sheets)

## 1.1 Water Workbook Scope & Parameters (`README`)
- DISSERTATION LITERATURE SYNTHESIS: WATER & AI DATA CENTRE EXPANSION IN INDIA
- Stage 3 Progress: Complete Multi-Tier Evidence Integration (Academic, Think-Tank, Policy, and Press Corpus)
- Master Workbook: water_datacentre_literature_synthesis_v1.xlsx | Generation Date: 16 November 2026
- 1. PURPOSE & RESEARCH QUESTIONS
- This workbook provides a systematic, mathematically verified, closed-sandbox evidence synthesis supporting a university dissertation on whether India's artificial intelligence (AI) and hyperscale data centre expansion is environmentally sustainable, focusing specifically on WATER consumption, withdrawal, and groundwater competition.
- Primary Research Questions:
- - RQ1: What is the direct and indirect water intensity of AI compute and modern data centre cooling systems across varying climates?
- - RQ2: To what extent are global and Indian data centres sited in baseline water-stressed and groundwater-critical basins?
- - RQ3: How do empirical water consumption realities align with corporate disclosures, state policy incentives, and local hydrological limits (e.g. CGWB 2025 assessment)?
- 2. WORKBOOK SHEET DIRECTORY & ARCHITECTURE
- The workbook contains 6 operational sheets structured according to the formal dissertation synthesis protocol:
- 1. README: Project purpose, sheet architecture, tagging conventions, verification rules, dates, and methodological limits.
- 2. Source_Register: Comprehensive master register of all 14 evaluated literature sources (P01 to P14) with full bibliographic, empirical, and access metadata.
- 3. Extraction: Granular evidence log containing 215 verbatim extracted rows across peer-reviewed, preprint, and think-tank sources (P01–P10).
- 4. India_Evidence: Filtered, clustered synthesis of 51 specific empirical and policy rows pertaining to Indian metro hubs and national frameworks.
- 5. India_Press: Dedicated investigation log containing 26 verbatim rows from Indian press investigations (P11 to P14), tracking local conflicts and regulatory files.
- 6. Checks: Automated code verification logs, P07 28-page numbering audit, line-break hyphen normalization log, and Paper x Variable Category (A to M) matrix.
- 3. CODES & TAGGING SYSTEM
- Strict ontological tagging is enforced across all extraction and analysis sheets to maintain epistemological rigor:
- - [S] = Stated directly in the source paper. Mandatory for every single row in the Extraction and India_Press sheets. No inference permitted.
- - [I] = Inference by the researcher. Permitted exclusively in analytical columns (e.g. 'source quality note [I]', 'Missing Categories [I]').
- - page_flag = 'PDF page only': Explicitly marks data extracted from unnumbered PDF pages (e.g. web-to-PDF printouts, unnumbered preprints, or front-matter).
- - statement_type taxonomy: measured | modelled estimate | projection | scenario | authors' opinion | stakeholder perspective | literature citation | press investigation.
- - Variable Categories (A to M): A (Definitions), B (Intensity metrics), C (Volumes/projections), D (Cooling/design), E (Water source), F (Location/stress), G (Siting drivers), H (Local impacts/competition), I (Governance/policy), J (Transparency), K (Mitigation/recycling), L (Methods/limits), M (India-specific findings).
- 4. STRICT VERIFICATION & EXTRACTION RULES
- - Excerpt Length & Quotation: Every row contains a continuous verbatim excerpt of <= 25 words enclosed in double quotes, verified by automated PyMuPDF code matching.
- - Line-Break Hyphen Normalization: Preserves real hyphens (e.g. 'process-based', 'water-stressed') while normalizing line-break word splits (e.g. 'utilit-\nies' -> 'utilities').
- - No Numbers Invented: Numerical values are extracted exactly as printed with original units and years. No unit conversions in Extraction.
- - Secondary Source Labeling: P07 citations (e.g. Mordor Intelligence), P12 (summarises CEEW, NITI Aayog), and P14 (summarises Rystad Energy) are explicitly marked as secondary.
- - Dissertation Dataset Independence: P13 uses CGWB 2025 Dynamic Resource Assessment data and is flagged as non-independent of the researcher's dataset.
- - P08 Status: Table 1 body and Figures 1 to 3 are not captured in the PDF; no outside number (such as India = 248) has been taken or inferred.
- - P07 Table 1 Rule: Table 1 state policy checkmarks are graphical/non-machine-readable and excluded to prevent artificial intelligence hallucination.
- 5. PUBLICATION DATES AS PRINTED
- - P01 (Mytton): 15 February 2021 (printed in npj Clean Water)
- - P02 (Siddik et al.): 21 May 2021 (printed in Environmental Research Letters)
- - P03 (Li et al.): 26 March 2025 (printed arXiv v5 stamp)
- - P04 (de Vries-Gao): 9 January 2026 (printed in Patterns journal footer)
- - P05 (Rosa et al.): DATE NOT PRINTED (web print-to-PDF; text cites studies up to 2026)
- - P06 (Masanet): DATE NOT PRINTED (arXiv preprint; text explicitly notes studies up to 15 September 2026 were included)
- - P07 (CEEW): February 2026 (printed on cover and executive summary)
- - P08 (Planet Tracker Asia): DATE NOT PRINTED (file carries a banner reading 'PLANET TRACKER (FINANCIAL & ENVIRONMENTAL THINK TANK) · MARCH 2025' and byline 'Published by Planet Tracker Research Team | March 2025'; not verified at the original website; DataCenterMap accessed 2 March 2025, pdf p. 2; no stable page; cite section and paragraph; pdf_page is locator in my file, not a page of the source)
- - P09 (Planet Tracker US): DATE NOT PRINTED (file carries a banner reading 'PLANET TRACKER (FINANCIAL & ENVIRONMENTAL THINK TANK) · SEPTEMBER 2024' and byline 'Published by Planet Tracker Research Team | September 2024'; not verified at the original website; no stable page; cite section and paragraph; pdf_page is locator in my file, not a page of the source)
- - P10 (Baringa): November 2025 (printed on document cover)
- - P11 to P14 (Press articles): DATE NOT PRINTED (files carry generated banners and bylines; not verified at the original websites; no stable page; cite section and paragraph; pdf_page is locator in my file, not a page of the source)

evaluated across US data centres mapped across river basins, EXT-148; Publication date: DATE NOT PRINTED [file carries banner 'PLANET TRACKER · SEPTEMBER 2024'; not verified at original website]; Metric: WRI Aqueduct water stress categories, EXT-148; Not modeled in P01–P14. The literature establishes the qualitative thermodynamic trade-off (P02, EXT-040; P06, EXT-110; P10, EXT-164), but the resulting grid water trade-off across India's regional power grids remains an unaddressed empirical question. |
| PAT-06 | Hyperscale facilities achieve significantly superior nominal water and power efficiency compared to enterprise facilities, but their massive absolute capacity creates severe localized hydrological point-source pressure. The literature reports two separate modeling benchmarks for a 100-MW facility (not a single range):<br>  (1) Daily benchmark: ~2 million litres of water per day for a typical 100-MW hyperscale facility (cited from IEA by P07, CEEW, pdf pp. 8, 13, EXT-197, EXT-201). In annual terms, this equates to derived [I], calculated from EXT-197 as: (2,000,000 L/day * 365 days) / 10^9 = 0.73 billion L/year.<br>  (2) Annual benchmark: 2.5 billion litres of water each year for a single 100-MW hyperscale facility using evaporative cooling—enough to supply more than 80,000 people (P10, Baringa, pdf p. 3, EXT-156; pdf p. 8, EXT-160).<br>These figures contrast with a medium 15-MW facility using water equivalent to 3 average hospitals or two 18-hole golf courses (P01, Mytton, pdf p. 1, EXT-007; Masanet, P06, pdf pp. 5, 12, EXT-111, EXT-114). | YES |  |  |  |  | YES |  |  |  | YES | 3 | P01, P06, P10 | EXT-007, EXT-013 (pdf p. 1, 2); EXT-111, EXT-114 (pdf p. 5, 12); EXT-156, EXT-161 (pdf p. 3) | P01, P06, and P10 independently evaluate hyperscale vs enterprise efficiency metrics and localized water withdrawal impacts. | No merging required: P01 (synthesis of US hyperscale PUEs vs enterprise), P06 (academic literature meta-analysis of data center scales), and P10 (consultancy engineering modeling of 100-MW facilities) represent 3 distinct independent lineages. Confirmed independent lineages = 3 (>=2). | EXT-161 (pdf p. 3, P10) notes that over 40% of newly announced hyperscale capacity since 2023 is shifting toward low- or no-water cooling designs, meaning future hyperscale campuses will not all rely on traditional evaporative cooling towers. | A 100-MW hyperscale facility consumes ~2 million litres of water per day (cited from IEA by P07, pdf p. 8, EXT-197; derived [I]: 0.73 billion L/year) or 2.5 billion litres annually under evaporative cooling (enough to supply more than 80,000 people, P10, pdf p. 3, EXT-156). In Indian metropolitan clusters, this creates localized point-source competition with municipal supplies and private tankers (P07, pdf pp. 8, 15, EXT-197, EXT-207). |

(P03) models prompt-level AI inference water consumption; other corpus studies evaluate annual facility or fleet totals. | Highly sensitive to server cluster location, PUE, and cooling system assumptions; cannot be generalized globally without dynamic telemetry. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SSF-02 | P05 | EXT-081 (pdf p. 1), EXT-094 (pdf p. 2), EXT-104 (pdf p. 5) | Global spatial allocation of 7,364 facilities across specific cooling types using machine-learning climate regression. | Only Rosa et al. uses machine-learning regression based on wet-bulb temperature to infer facility cooling technologies globally. | Unverified against ground-truth operator disclosures; provides valuable macro-spatial insight but carries individual facility classification uncertainty. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SSF-03 | P10 | EXT-152 (pdf p. 2), EXT-167 (pdf p. 6), EXT-172 (pdf p. 8) | Commercial Water Purchase Agreements (WPAs) and Priority Planning Zones (PPZs) as structured contracting tools. | Only Baringa proposes WPAs and PPZs as structured contracting tools for data centre water procurement and regulatory zoning. | Forward-looking commercial advisory framework rather than an empirical study; represents industry perspective on future risk mitigation. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| SSF-05 | P14 [SECONDARY] | PRS-024 (pdf p. 2) citing Rystad Energy 2026 | Operator definitional divergence: AWS and Meta define WUE as withdrawal, whereas Microsoft and Google define WUE as consumption. | Only reported in secondary press (P14 / Down To Earth citing Rystad Energy); no primary academic paper from P01-P10 confirms this cross-operator distinction. | Moved from Definitions_Compared as required by Check 3. Analytical critique [I]: If AWS/Meta report withdrawal, their reported WUE is inflated relative to Microsoft/Google, destroying cross-corporate comparability. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SSF-06 | P10 | EXT-164 (pdf p. 4), EXT-165 (pdf p. 5), EXT-171 (pdf p. 8) | Dry air cooling electricity penalty: cuts water use close to zero but increases data centre energy consumption by 20% to 40% compared to evaporative systems (rising to ~40% in hot, arid climates; P10, Baringa 2025, pdf p. 4, EXT-164; pdf p. 8, EXT-171). This specific 20% to 40% numerical range is a single-source finding from P10 only, unconfirmed by other papers in the corpus. | P10 (Baringa 2025) provides the only quantified 20% to 40% electricity uplift factor for dry cooling. | Commercial advisory report; provides engineering rule-of-thumb estimate rather than empirical metered operational telemetry across diverse facility designs. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

## 1.6 Water Contradictions, Disagreements & Evidence Gaps
> _CONTRADICTIONS, DISAGREEMENTS & SYSTEMATIC EVIDENCE GAPS_

> _Rigorous Cross-Examination of Conflicting Estimates, System Boundary Asymmetries, and Literature Voids [Analytical Inferences Marked [I]]_

| issue_id | topic | value_a_and_source | value_b_and_source | nature_of_contradiction_or_methodological_cause_[I] | reconciliation_or_verdict_[I] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DIS-01 | Global Annual Data Centre Direct Water Consumption | 140 billion L direct water consumption globally in 2023 (P04, EXT-070, pdf p. 5 citing IEA 2024) | >560 billion L in 2024 (P10, EXT-155, pdf p. 3; P07, EXT-195, pdf p. 7 citing IEA 2025) | IEA 2024 estimated 140 billion L direct water on 360 TWh electricity demand (P04, EXT-070, pdf p. 5); Baringa (P10, EXT-155) reports >560 billion L globally in 2024, rising to 1.2 trillion L by 2030. | Top-down global intensity benchmarks severely underestimate real-world evaporative cooling demand by ignoring local climate extremes and peak summer temperatures. |
| DIS-02 | Direct Operational WUEsite Benchmarks Across Facilities | AWS direct site WUE = 0.12 L/kWh, Meta = 0.19 L/kWh, Microsoft = 0.27 L/kWh (P14, PRS-024, pdf p. 2 citing Rystad Energy 2026) | Google average direct WUE = 1.04 L/kWh (P04, EXT-075, pdf p. 7); Equinix direct WUE = 0.91 L/kWh (P14, PRS-024, pdf p. 2) | Conflation of system boundaries (withdrawal vs consumption), distinct climate zones of data centre fleets, and differing cooling architectures (direct evaporative vs chilled water vs air-cooled chillers). Masanet notes studies frequently fail to disclose WUE sources (P06, EXT-129, EXT-130, pdf p. 117). | A single universal WUE benchmark cannot be applied across operators or geographies without adjusting for local wet-bulb temperature, economizer hours, and reporting boundary. |
| DIS-03 | Share of Data Centres Located in Water-Stressed Areas | 20% of US data centre direct water from stressed subbasins (P02, EXT-022, pdf p. 3 using AWARE); 25% of global data centres face water gaps (P05, EXT-082, pdf p. 1) | 32% of US facilities in high/extremely high stress (P09, EXT-147, pdf p. 3; explicitly United States, not global); 41% of Asian data centres in extremely high stress (P08, EXT-182, pdf p. 2); 52% of global hubs in water-stressed regions (P10, EXT-158, pdf p. 3) | Differences in geographic scope (US vs Global vs Asia), differing hydrological stress indices (ISO 14046 AWARE vs monthly water gaps vs WRI Aqueduct BWS), and varying stress thresholds (40% vs 80%). | When evaluated using identical stress metrics, Asian and Indian data centres face nearly double the extreme water stress exposure of North American facilities. |
| DIS-04 | System Boundary Accounting: Water Withdrawal vs. Water Consumption | Consumption (evaporative loss from local watershed) is the essential metric for assessing hydrological resource depletion (P01, EXT-008, pdf p. 1; P02, EXT-026, pdf p. 4; P04, EXT-068, pdf p. 5; P06, EXT-106, pdf p. 1) | Corporate disclosures and utility reporting frequently measure Withdrawal (total water intake, including return flows), which is easier to meter but conflates non-consumptive cooling with net loss (P03, EXT-048, pdf p. 2; P09, EXT-133, pdf p. 2; P14, PRS-024, pdf p. 2) | Operators report withdrawal to align with municipal utility billing meters or claim water circularity, while hydrologists require consumption to evaluate local watershed mass balances. | Conflating withdrawal and consumption distorts water footprint evaluations because withdrawal includes recirculated or returned volumes (P01, pdf p. 1, EXT-008; P06, pdf p. 1, EXT-106). Studies emphasize that consumption (evaporative loss) is the critical parameter for local watershed stress, whereas withdrawal metrics overstate on-site depletion while obscuring actual hydrological balance (P02, pdf p. 4, EXT-026; P06, pdf pp. 1, 18, EXT-106, EXT-116). |
| **2. SYSTEMATIC LITERATURE GAPS (PAPERS AUDITED ACROSS CORPUS)** | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| gap_id | gap_description | papers_checked | evidence_state_in_corpus | significance_for_india_dissertation_[I] |  |
| GAP-01 | Absence of Empirical Facility-Level Water Telemetry in India and Global South | P01, P02, P03, P04, P05, P06, P07, P08, P09, P10 | All quantitative facility models are US- or EU-centric; P07 is qualitative/stakeholder-based; P08 is regional text only; no empirical sensor telemetry exists for Indian data centres. | Forces Indian policymakers to rely on unverified corporate CSR claims or imported temperate-climate benchmarks that understate tropical cooling needs. |  |
| GAP-02 | Omission of Seasonal and Diurnal Hydrological Stress Dynamics | P01, P02, P05, P06, P08, P09, P10 | Studies rely on annual baseline stress indices (WRI Aqueduct BWS or annual AWARE); only P05 models monthly water gaps, but no study integrates diurnal peak cooling during summer heatwaves. | In India's monsoon-dependent climate, annual averages mask severe 4-month pre-monsoon water deficits when data centre cooling demand peaks. |  |
| GAP-03 | Lack of Disaggregation Across Water Source Types (Groundwater vs Municipal vs Tanker) | P01, P02, P04, P05, P06, P07, P09, P10 | Corporate disclosures aggregate water into a single volumetric number; only P07 qualitatively notes reliance on private water tankers and borewells in Chennai and Bengaluru. | Prevents direct attribution of data centre cooling to localized groundwater table depletion and aquifer over-extraction in Indian metropolitan fringes. |  |
| GAP-04 | Total Omission of Scope 3 Embodied Water (Semiconductor Fabrication & Construction) | P01, P02, P03, P04, P05, P06, P07, P08, P09, P10 | P03 and P06 explicitly mention Scope 3 embodied water in their conceptual taxonomy (EXT-055, EXT-110), but neither quantifies it; no paper calculates embodied water in chip fabrication. | True lifecycle water footprint of AI expansion is substantially higher than operational metrics reflect, obscuring global water transfer embodied in AI accelerator imports. |  |
| GAP-05 | Lack of Water Quality and Thermal Discharge Degradation Analysis | P01, P02, P05, P06, P07, P10 | Cooling tower blowdown water carries high mineral concentration and biocide chemicals; no paper evaluates downstream ecological or municipal wastewater treatment impacts. | Blowdown discharge in water-scarce Indian cities risks degrading municipal wastewater infrastructure and contaminating surrounding surface water bodies. |  |
| **3. WEAKEST POINTS OF EVIDENCE BASE IN CORPUS** | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| weakness_id | weakness_description | contributing_factors | implication_for_research_[I] |  |  |
| WKP-01 | Secondary Citation Cascades & Circular Evidence Loops | Multiple studies cite older benchmark figures (e.g. Shehabi et al. 2016 for US totals, or IEA 2024 for global aggregates) rather than generating fresh primary empirical data. | Creates an artificial impression of robust empirical consensus when the entire literature rests on a handful of foundational modeling papers. |  |  |
| WKP-02 | Inferential Cooling Technology Modeling Without Ground-Truth Verification | Studies assign cooling systems to facilities via algorithmic proxy (e.g. P05 using climate regression) rather than verified ground-truth data centre equipment registers. | Macro-level regional aggregates are statistically robust, but individual facility allocations carry significant uncertainty. |  |  |
| WKP-03 | Complete Opacity of Colocation and Wholesale Data Centres | Colocation facilities host substantial data centre capacity yet disclose virtually zero public water metrics due to multi-tenant split incentives and lack of measurement (P01 notes less than a third of operators measure water consumption, EXT-003, pdf p. 1; P02 notes absence of public commercial facility data, EXT-038, pdf p. 11; P06, EXT-125, pdf p. 48; P07, EXT-205, pdf p. 14). | Literature is heavily skewed toward self-owned hyperscale campuses (Google, Microsoft, Meta), leaving the fastest-growing market segment completely unmonitored. |  |  |

## 1.8 Link to Dissertation Dataset: Hydrological Indicators & District Audit
> _LINK TO DISSERTATION DATASET: HYDROLOGICAL INDICATOR SYNTHESIS & DISTRICT AUDIT_

> _Methodological Comparison of CGWB Groundwater Metrics vs Literature Stress Indices [Inferences Marked [I]; P13 Non-Confirmation Enforced]_

| indicator_id | dissertation_indicator_name | dissertation_formula_and_unit | literature_counterpart_metric | paper_citations_and_locators | measured_the_same_way?_[I] | methodological_divergence_and_system_boundary_[I] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IND-01 | Stage of Groundwater Extraction (SOE) (%) | Total Annual Groundwater Draft / Net Annual Extractable Groundwater Availability * 100 (Unit: %) | Water Scarcity Footprint (AWARE CF > 1.0) in P02; Baseline Water Stress (BWS > 40%) in P05, P07, P08, P09, P10 | EXT-022, EXT-030 (pdf p. 3, 7, P02); EXT-089 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-192 (pdf p. 5, P07); EXT-181 (pdf p. 1, P08); EXT-148 (pdf p. 3, P09); EXT-158 (pdf p. 3, P10) | NO [I] | Fundamental ontological divergence: CGWB SOE measures strictly sub-surface groundwater extraction relative to dynamic annual recharge in an administrative boundary. Literature BWS measures total consumptive withdrawal (surface rivers/lakes + groundwater) relative to total mean renewable flow in a hydrological basin. P02 uses AWARE characterization factors (relative available water remaining per area). The metrics cannot be treated as equivalent [I]. |
| IND-02 | District Groundwater Categorization | Categorical status based on SOE and long-term pre/post monsoon water level trends: Safe (<=70%), Semi-Critical (70-90%), Critical (90-100%), Over-Exploited (>100%) | Water stress intensity tiers: Low (<10%), Low-Medium (10-20%), Medium-High (20-40%), High (40-80%), Extremely High (>80%) in WRI Aqueduct | EXT-089 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-192 (pdf p. 5, P07); EXT-181 (pdf p. 1, P08); EXT-147, EXT-148 (pdf p. 3, P09) | NO [I] | CGWB categorization incorporates 10-year empirical water-table trend analysis alongside extraction ratio. WRI Aqueduct uses static modeled withdrawal-to-availability ratio. An Over-Exploited CGWB district represents physical sub-surface aquifer depletion (>100% recharge drafted), whereas WRI High Stress triggers at only 40% surface/groundwater withdrawal ratio [I]. |
| IND-03 | Annual Extractable Groundwater Resource (Ham / MCM) | Total annual replenishable recharge minus natural unextractable baseflow during non-monsoon season (Unit: Hectare-metre [Ham] = 0.01 MCM) | Renewable freshwater availability (mean annual surface runoff + subbasin groundwater flow) | EXT-026 (pdf p. 4, P02); EXT-084 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-093 (pdf p. 2, P05); EXT-192 (pdf p. 5, P07) | NO [I] | CGWB assesses extractable groundwater using ground-level water-level fluctuation (WLF) and rainfall infiltration factor (RIF) methods. Literature studies assess basin-wide renewable streamflow and hydrological runoff via gridded macro-hydrological models (e.g. PCR-GLOBWB in P05). CGWB reflects local hydrogeological storage; literature reflects river basin discharge [I]. |
| IND-04 | Annual Industrial Groundwater Draft (Ham / MCM) | Gross annual groundwater extraction specifically allocated to industrial manufacturing and commercial establishments (Unit: Ham = 0.01 MCM) | Direct operational water consumption & withdrawal for commercial data centres | EXT-002, EXT-006 (pdf p. 1, P01); EXT-033 (pdf p. 8, P02); EXT-088 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-188, EXT-197 (pdf p. 2, 8, P07) | PARTIAL [I] | CGWB industrial draft aggregates all industrial sectors (textiles, chemical, manufacturing, IT) based on borehole metering or commercial power connections. In the literature, data centre water is tracked as a dedicated facility parameter. Data centres represent an unmetered subset within CGWB industrial draft, often disguised within municipal or commercial utility connections [I]. |
| IND-05 | Annual Domestic & Agricultural Groundwater Drafts (Ham / MCM) | Gross annual groundwater extraction for rural/urban drinking supply (Domestic) and crop irrigation (Agricultural) (Unit: Ham) | Sectoral water competition: Municipal public supply overlap (41%) and Irrigation overlap (20%) in data center basins | EXT-084 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-103, EXT-104 (pdf p. 5, P05); EXT-191, EXT-198 (pdf p. 4, 8, P07) | YES (Conceptually) [I] | Both track sectoral allocations. P05 explicitly models spatial overlap between data centres and municipal (41%) and irrigation (20%) water demands, proving that in water-scarce basins, data center cooling directly competes with urban drinking water and rural irrigation—mirroring the trade-offs captured in CGWB district draft allocations [I]. |
| IND-06 | District Operational & Planned Data Centre IT Capacity (MW) | Aggregated IT electrical power capacity of operational, under-construction, and planned data centre facilities by district (Unit: Megawatts [MW]) | Data center facility power capacity (MW) and IT power load used to scale water consumption models | EXT-007 (pdf p. 1, P01); EXT-028 (pdf p. 5, P02); EXT-156 (pdf p. 3, P10); EXT-188, EXT-197 (pdf p. 2, 8, P07) | YES [I] | Measured identically: IT load in MW is the universal engineering scaling metric in both the dissertation dataset and literature models. Literature establishes that a 100-MW facility requires ~2 million litres of cooling water daily (cited from IEA by P07, EXT-197, pdf p. 8; derived [I]: 0.73 billion L/year) or 2.5 billion litres annually under evaporative cooling (P10, EXT-156, pdf p. 3). Facility electricity consumption scales as derived [I], calculated as 100 MW * 24 h * 1,000 kW/MW = 2,400,000 kWh/day. |
| **2. SYSTEMATIC COMPARISON: CGWB STAGE OF EXTRACTION VS. WRI AQUEDUCT BASELINE WATER STRESS** | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| comparison_dimension | cgwb_stage_of_groundwater_extraction_(soe) | wri_aqueduct_baseline_water_stress_(bws) | supporting_literature_citations | methodological_difference_verdict_[I] |  |  |
| Numerator (Demand Parameter) | Annual gross groundwater extraction across irrigation, domestic, and industrial sectors only (strictly sub-surface aquifer draft). | Total freshwater withdrawal / consumptive demand across municipal, industrial, and agricultural sectors (surface river/reservoir + groundwater). | EXT-192 (pdf p. 5, P07); EXT-009 (pdf p. 2, P01); EXT-089 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-148 (pdf p. 3, P09) | WRI captures all surface water and groundwater diverted from local ecosystems; CGWB isolates sub-surface borehole pumping. A district relying heavily on piped surface reservoirs (e.g. coastal metropolitan areas) can exhibit low CGWB groundwater SOE while experiencing high basin-level WRI water stress (EXT-081, EXT-192) [I]. |  |  |
| Denominator (Supply Parameter) | Net annual extractable groundwater resource (annual replenishable rainfall recharge + canal seepage minus environmental baseflow/natural discharge). | Total available renewable freshwater supply (mean annual freshwater flow / runoff from catchment rivers, lakes, and shallow aquifers). | EXT-026 (pdf p. 4, P02); EXT-093, EXT-094 (pdf p. 2, P05); EXT-178, EXT-181 (pdf p. 1, 2, P08) | CGWB measures localized hydrogeological aquifer storage and recharge capacity. WRI measures watershed-scale hydrological streamflow. Denominators represent completely distinct physical hydrological reservoirs and cannot be mathematically reconciled [I]. |  |  |
| Temporal Resolution & Seasonality | Static annual water-year balance: computed once annually by comparing post-monsoon and pre-monsoon piezometer groundwater levels. | Monthly-weighted baseline: models seasonal hydrologic variability across 12 months, incorporating upstream dam flow regulation and dry-month runoff deficits. | EXT-082, EXT-093 (pdf p. 1, 2, P05); EXT-120 (pdf p. 30, P06) | WRI captures critical 4-month pre-monsoon dry season deficits when data centre cooling demand is highest; CGWB annual averaging dilutes peak summer groundwater distress by averaging it with heavy monsoon recharge [I]. |  |  |
| Spatial Unit of Assessment | Administrative boundaries: Taluks, Assessment Units, Blocks, and Revenue Districts demarcated by state governments. | Hydrological boundaries: HydroBASINS Pfafstetter level 6 catchment subbasins demarcated by topography and natural river divides. | EXT-028 (pdf p. 5, P02); EXT-081 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-188 (pdf p. 2, P07) | Administrative district boundaries crosscut multiple distinct hydrogeological aquifers and watershed divides; an administrative district may be 'Safe' on average while hosting severe localized aquifer over-exploitation in an industrial sub-pocket [I]. |  |  |
| Risk Thresholds & Policy Meaning | Safe (<=70%), Semi-Critical (70-90%), Critical (90-100%), Over-Exploited (>100%). Over-Exploited means extraction exceeds net annual recharge. | Low (<10%), Low-Medium (10-20%), Medium-High (20-40%), High (40-80%), Extremely High (>80%). High stress begins at 40% withdrawal-to-availability ratio. | EXT-089 (pdf p. 1, P05); EXT-181 (pdf p. 1, P08); EXT-192 (pdf p. 5, P07); EXT-158 (pdf p. 3, P10) | A district reaching CGWB Over-Exploited status (>100%) represents physical aquifer depletion and imminent well-failure. In contrast, WRI High Stress triggers at only 40% withdrawal ratio. They represent completely different physical thresholds and policy implications [I]. |  |  |

| missing_indicator_name | metric_definition_and_unit | source_paper_ids_and_locators | why_unavailable_in_district_dataset_[I] | analytical_proxy_strategy_for_dissertation_[I] |  |  |
| Facility-Level Water Usage Effectiveness (WUEsite) | Annual direct facility water consumption (L) divided by IT equipment energy consumption (kWh) (Unit: L/kWh) | EXT-011, EXT-017 (pdf p. 2, 3, P01); EXT-048 (pdf p. 2, P03); EXT-075 (pdf p. 7, P04); EXT-114 (pdf p. 12, P06); EXT-197 (pdf p. 8, P07) | Data centre operators in India treat facility water consumption as strictly proprietary commercial information; zero public facility-level telemetry exists in regulatory filings or state portals. | Apply literature benchmarks (such as Google 1.04 L/kWh from P04, EXT-075, or CEEW benchmark 0.83 L/kWh derived [I] from P07, EXT-197 as: 2,000,000 L/day / (100 MW * 24 h * 1,000 kW/MW)) across operational MW capacity to model estimated district cooling water demand [I]. |  |  |
| Facility Cooling System Architecture | Categorization of on-site heat rejection: Evaporative cooling towers, direct evaporative cooling, air-cooled chillers, hybrid chillers, liquid immersion | EXT-011, EXT-015 (pdf p. 2, 3, P01); EXT-021 (pdf p. 3, P02); EXT-085, EXT-105 (pdf p. 1, 6, P05); EXT-111, EXT-112 (pdf p. 5, 8, P06); EXT-190 (pdf p. 3, P07) | Facility mechanical equipment registers are proprietary; state environmental impact clearances (SEAC) specify cooling type vaguely without detailing wet-bulb economizer thresholds. | Use local district summer wet-bulb temperatures and coastal vs inland geography as proxies: coastal hubs (Mumbai, Chennai) rely heavily on wet evaporative towers; dry inland hubs use hybrid/air-cooled chillers [I]. |  |  |
| Water Source Breakdown by Facility (Municipal vs Borewell vs Tanker) | Volumetric percentage of facility water supplied via piped municipal connections, on-site groundwater borewells, and private water tankers (%) | EXT-014 (pdf p. 2, P01); EXT-026 (pdf p. 4, P02); EXT-069 (pdf p. 5, P04); EXT-170 (pdf p. 7, P10); EXT-191, EXT-198 (pdf p. 4, 8, P07) | Municipal water utilities in India do not publish metered industrial consumption broken down by consumer; informal private water tanker deliveries operate in unmonitored shadow markets (qualitatively observed by P07, EXT-198, EXT-207, pdf p. 8, 16). | Cross-reference municipal water deficit days in district with CEEW qualitative findings (P07): districts with municipal supply deficits (Bengaluru, Chennai) inherently shift cooling load to groundwater borewells and tankers [I]. |  |  |
| Diurnal & Seasonal Water Metering Traces | High-frequency (hourly/monthly) water consumption data capturing pre-monsoon peak summer evaporative cooling spikes (Unit: L/hour or L/month) | EXT-082, EXT-093, EXT-102 (pdf p. 1, 2, 4, P05); EXT-120 (pdf p. 30, P06) | CGWB publishes single annual water-year estimates; state groundwater departments do not deploy real-time digital flow meters on commercial data centre wells. | Model seasonal evaporative multiplier using monthly climatological wet-bulb temperature variations: scale baseline WUE by 1.4x to 1.8x during pre-monsoon summer months (March-May) following P05 methodology [I]. |  |  |
| Cooling Tower Cycles of Concentration (CoC) & Blowdown Discharge | Ratio of dissolved solids in blowdown water to makeup water, determining water circularity and discharge volume (Dimensionless ratio: 3 to 7) | EXT-110, EXT-111 (pdf p. 4, 5, P06); EXT-210 (pdf p. 18, P07) | Facility water treatment chemistry and blowdown disposal logs are confidential; state pollution control boards (SPCB) monitor only gross effluent discharge norms. | What cycles of concentration (CoC) and blowdown treatment rates are achieved in Indian data centre cooling towers? Not reported in P01–P14. Future investigation: Does raw water quality in Indian metros limit CoC, thereby increasing makeup water demand above temperate literature baselines (P06, pdf p. 4, 5, EXT-110, EXT-111)? |  |  |
| Indirect Scope 2 Power Grid Water Consumption Factor by Regional Discom | Liters of water consumed at off-site thermal and hydro power stations supplying electricity to the specific data center grid (Unit: L/kWh) | EXT-009, EXT-010 (pdf p. 2, P01); EXT-028, EXT-034 (pdf p. 5, 8, P02); EXT-076 (pdf p. 7, P04); EXT-116 (pdf p. 18, P06); EXT-189 (pdf p. 2, P07) | State electricity regulatory commissions (SERCs) and power discoms publish coal-mix data but do not report plant-specific water consumption intensities. | What is the off-site water intensity of electricity supplied to data centres by regional Indian power grids? Not reported in P01–P14 (CEA norms are outside literature scope). Future research question: Can regional grid thermal vs renewable generation mixes be coupled with generation water intensity factors (P01, EXT-009; P02, EXT-028; P06, EXT-116) to estimate indirect district water burdens? |  |  |
| **4. CRITICAL RESEARCH QUESTIONS THE LITERATURE SUGGESTS ASKING OF EACH INDIAN DISTRICT** | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| question_id | research_question_for_indian_district | theoretical_justification_from_literature | supporting_row_ids_and_locators | how_to_evaluate_with_my_dataset_[I] |  |  |
| Q-01 | Is data centre capacity disproportionately concentrated in districts where groundwater extraction exceeds 100% (Over-Exploited) or 90% (Critical)? | Literature across US, Europe, and Asia proves that data centre siting is historically decoupled from hydrological availability, clustering in water-stressed basins (P02, P08, P10). | EXT-022 (pdf p. 3, P02); EXT-158 (pdf p. 3, P10); EXT-181 (pdf p. 2, P08); EXT-192, EXT-201 (pdf p. 5, 10, P07) | Overlay district operational and pipeline MW capacity against CGWB Stage of Extraction (SOE); calculate the exact percentage of state IT capacity located in Over-Exploited vs Safe districts [I]. |  |  |
| Q-02 | What share of the district's annual industrial groundwater allocation would be consumed if all planned data centre campuses operate with evaporative cooling? | Literature establishes that a 100-MW facility requires ~2 million litres of water per day (cited from IEA by P07, EXT-197, pdf p. 8; converted to 0.73 million m3/year as derived [I], calculated from EXT-197: 2,000,000 * 365 / 10^6) or 2.5 billion litres annually under evaporative cooling (P10, EXT-156, pdf p. 3; equivalent to 2.5 million m3/year), which can compete with local industrial water allocations in non-industrial districts. | EXT-007 (pdf p. 1, P01); EXT-156 (pdf p. 3, P10); EXT-188, EXT-197 (pdf p. 2, 8, P07) | Compute modelled data centre annual cooling water demand using CEEW derived benchmark (0.83 L/kWh derived [I] from P07, EXT-197) multiplied by MW capacity and 8760 h/year, and divide by CGWB Annual Industrial Groundwater Draft (converted from Ham to MCM) [I]. |  |  |
| Q-03 | Does the district host submarine cable landing stations or primary power substations that create infrastructure lock-in overriding water stress? | Literature documents that data centre developers prioritize low latency and grid availability above all else, accepting acute water resilience risks to cluster near landing points (P07, P10). | EXT-163 (pdf p. 4, P10); EXT-188, EXT-198, EXT-201 (pdf p. 2, 8, 10, P07) | Code districts as coastal landing hubs (Mumbai, Chennai), inland transit hubs (Bengaluru, Hyderabad), or peripheral nodes; test whether IT capacity correlates stronger with landing stations than water availability [I]. |  |  |
| Q-04 | What is the vulnerability of the district's data centre cluster to extreme summer pre-monsoon water deficits (March to June)? | Rosa et al. (P05) prove that annual water stress indices mask acute seasonal water gaps when cooling tower water demand peaks exactly when water availability hits its annual minimum. | EXT-082, EXT-093, EXT-102 (pdf p. 1, 2, 4, P05); EXT-198, EXT-207 (pdf p. 8, 16, P07) | Examine pre-monsoon groundwater level drop (m bgl) from CGWB piezometer data in data centre clusters; identify districts with pre-monsoon water table declines >4 metres [I]. |  |  |
| Q-05 | Are data centre campuses in the district relying on informal private water tanker deliveries to buffer against municipal utility supply failures? | CEEW documents that Indian data centres in Chennai and Bengaluru face periodic municipal piped water rationing, forcing reliance on unmonitored private tanker markets drawn from peri-urban borewells (P07). | EXT-191, EXT-198, EXT-207 (pdf p. 4, 8, 16, P07) | Identify districts with documented municipal water supply rationing or tanker reliance (as qualitatively reported for Chennai and Bengaluru by P07, EXT-198, EXT-207, pdf p. 8, 16); evaluate whether data centre growth in these districts shifts cooling water demand onto surrounding peri-urban groundwater aquifers [I]. |  |  |
| Q-06 | What is the indirect water consumption embodied in the electricity supplied by the state discom powering data centres in this district? | Literature demonstrates that indirect electricity-embedded water exceeds direct cooling water by 2x to 9x (P02, P04, P06); coal-dominated state grids create massive off-site water footprints. | EXT-009 (pdf p. 2, P01); EXT-034 (pdf p. 8, P02); EXT-076 (pdf p. 7, P04); EXT-116 (pdf p. 18, P06); EXT-189 (pdf p. 2, P07) | Examine the thermal vs renewable generation profile of the regional power grid serving the district; evaluate off-site water intensity using literature ranges (derived [I] multiplier: 2.95x direct water in P02, EXT-033, EXT-034; 8.75x in P04, EXT-070, EXT-076; Masanet, P06, EXT-116) [I]. |  |  |
| Q-07 | Does the state data centre policy governing this district mandate Treated Sewage Effluent (TSE) or Zero Liquid Discharge (ZLD) for facility cooling? | CEEW shows that state policies heavily incentivize power tariffs and land but rarely mandate water recycling standards, creating regulatory gaps in municipal water protection (P07). | EXT-209, EXT-210, EXT-211, EXT-213 (pdf p. 18, 19, P07) | Audit state data centre policy against provisions noted in P07 (e.g. Rajasthan 2025 mandating ZLD, EXT-210, pdf p. 18, versus state policies that offer power subsidies without water circularity mandates, EXT-209, EXT-213, pdf p. 18, 19); identify policy risk tier by district [I]. |  |  |
| Q-08 | How many residents' daily domestic drinking water allocation does the district's operational data centre capacity equate to? | Literature evaluates data centre water consumption using municipal population equivalents to illustrate local community competition and social license to operate (P01, P10). | EXT-007 (pdf p. 1, P01); EXT-156, EXT-160 (pdf p. 3, P10); EXT-197 (pdf p. 8, P07) | Compare modelled district data centre water consumption against direct literature human-scale benchmarks: a 100-MW facility equates to the annual water consumption of over 80,000 people (P10, pdf p. 3, EXT-156), or a 15-MW facility consumes as much water as three hospitals (P01, pdf p. 1, EXT-007) [I]. |  |  |

# Part 2: Energy & Grid Literature Synthesis (Cross-Cutting Sheets)

## 2.1 Energy Workbook Parameters & Boundaries (`README`)
| Section | Parameter | Description / Value | Notes & Methodological Boundaries |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Metadata | Workbook File Name | energy_datacentre_literature_synthesis_v1.xlsx | Master audited workbook for dissertation literature review |
| Metadata | Workbook Full Path | c:\Users\rajch\Desktop\SEM 7\Dissertation\01_Literature_Review\Energy\energy_datacentre_literature_synthesis_v1.xlsx | Direct absolute local workspace path |
| Metadata | Creation Timestamp | 2026-11-17 01:49:39 | Generated via automated PyMuPDF and openpyxl synthesis pipeline |
| Metadata | Version | v1.0 (Audited Master Workbook) | Stage 1 baseline containing verified extraction for E01-E04 |
| Metadata | Dissertation Scope | India Data Centre Expansion: Energy, Power & Grid Sustainability | Focusing on grid integration, substation constraints, peak demand, captive power |
| Conventions | [S] Tag | Stated directly in source text | Fact, number, or finding explicitly present in the source; zero extrapolation |
| Conventions | [I] Tag | Inference / Interpretation | Quarantined methodological interpretation, synthesis deduction, or quality score |
| Conventions | NOT REPORTED | Value or dimension not stated in source | Strictly recorded when a variable or parameter is absent; no outside guessing |
| Conventions | SECONDARY Tag | Web / Press / Trade Summary source | Flagged so source cannot count as independent evidence in synthesis patterns |
| Sheet Guide | Source_Register | Comprehensive inventory of all 24 literature sources (E01-E19) | 15 standard metadata columns including APA 7, methods, metrics, funding |
| Sheet Guide | Extraction | Granular verified data extractions (target 15-30 rows per full source) | 20 columns including exact page, <=25-word excerpt, statement type, author caveat |
| Sheet Guide | Variable_Matrix | Cross-study comparative matrix across key energy dimensions | Cells contain printed value + '(pdf p. X, row_id)' locator |
| Sheet Guide | Normalised_Values | Harmonised metrics with Excel conversion formulas | Common units: TWh/yr, GW, share (%). IT vs facility boundaries kept strictly separate |
| Sheet Guide | Definitions_Compared | Taxonomy of data centre load, capacity, contracted demand, geography | Identifies comparability distortions and boundary misalignments [I] |
| Sheet Guide | Patterns | Neutral empirical patterns with independent source counts | COUNTIF formulas, lineage tracing to prevent double-counting, counter-evidence |
| Sheet Guide | Contradictions_and_Gaps | Direct empirical disagreements, justified literature gaps, weak evidence | Explains divergence drivers [I] (method, boundary, vintage, geography) |
| Sheet Guide | Evidence_Quality | Systematic quality scoring (0 to 2) across 8 dimensions | Peer review, data sources, method, boundaries, limitations, India data, independence, reproducibility |
| Sheet Guide | Case_Studies | Detailed chronological timelines for Ashburn, Dublin, and India | Includes formal test of Ashburn '50 million sq ft idle capacity' claim |
| Sheet Guide | Link_to_My_Dataset | Direct mapping between empirical sources and user's dissertation indicators | Evaluates comparability [I], unheld source indicators, and state inquiry questions |
| Sheet Guide | India_Evidence | Consolidated ledger of all India-specific extractions | Traces origin, merged row_ids, and quality notes [I] across Indian states and hubs |
| Sheet Guide | Web_Press | Granular extractions from web, press, and trade reports | Includes paragraph numbers, secondary flags, and commercial bias assessments |
| Sheet Guide | Checks | Mechanical verification metrics, COUNTIFS category matrix, audit logs | Validates excerpt lengths, page locators, recalculation consistency |
| Sheet Guide | Decisions_Log | Transparent audit trail of conservative research decisions | Documents issues where text was unclear and conservative rules applied |
| Sheet Guide | Progress_Log | Chronological record of multi-stage execution and backups | Logs timestamps, sources processed, rows added, errors resolved, and backups saved |
| Verification Rules | PyMuPDF Automated Verification | 100% code check of all excerpts against PDF text | Verifies verbatim character sequence on stated page before row is accepted |
| Verification Rules | Excerpt Word Count Cap | Maximum 25 words per excerpt | Strict enforcement of dissertation protocol excerpt brevity |
| Verification Rules | Backup Safeguard | Pre-stage backup preservation | Never overwrites previous stage backups; preserved as stageN_backup.xlsx |

| E06 | Metered utility load (MW/GWh) vs requested capacity (MW) (pdf p. 45, ROW-0096, ROW-0097; pdf p. 69, ROW-0101) | Virginia hosts 13% of global and 25% of Americas operational data center capacity (pdf p. 5, ROW-0084) | Unconstrained electric demand in Virginia projected to double within 10 years (pdf p. 6, ROW-0085) | Data centers driving majority of Virginia load growth; small facilities draw 18 MW, largest draw 100-200+ MW (pdf p. 45, ROW-0096, ROW-0097) | VCEA requires 100% clean power by 2050; relies on unproven advanced nuclear technologies (pdf p. 8, ROW-0089; pdf p. 47, ROW-0098) | Near-flat 24/7 demand profile; high load factor creates continuous base-load strain on substations (pdf p. 45, ROW-0096, ROW-0097) | Interzonal transmission into Dominion zone must increase ~40%; out-of-state power imports double (pdf p. 50, ROW-0099, ROW-0100) | Unconstrained demand requires adding 1,500 MW gas plant every 2 yrs for 15 yrs or doubling solar/wind build rate (pdf p. 7, ROW-0087; pdf p. 8, ROW-0088) | Clustered heavily in Northern Virginia (Loudoun, Prince William); moving to rural co-op territories (pdf p. 5, ROW-0084; pdf p. 70, ROW-0102) | Dominion residential customer bills increase $14 to $37/month by 2040; co-ops face solvency risks (pdf p. 9, ROW-0091; pdf p. 70, ROW-0102) | Utilities can delay large load additions for transmission reliability; sales tax exemption saved $700M+ (pdf p. 9, ROW-0090; pdf p. 12, ROW-0094) | Dominion builds transmission only for forecasted metered load (1,000 MW) rather than requested capacity (2,000 MW) (pdf p. 69, ROW-0101) | Econometric modeling relies on utility load forecasts subject to market shift risks (pdf p. 6, ROW-0085) |

## 2.5 Multi-Paper Patterns, Single-Lineage Claims & Lineage Dependence Table (Energy)
| pattern_id | statement | category | primary_supporting_sources | illustrative_secondary_sources | lineage_groups (Note: "independent lineage group" means "separate publisher with no shared authors, no commissioning link, and no shared data found in the text") | num_independent_lineage_groups | primary_row_ids | primary_pdf_pages | independence_check | num_disagree_or_qualify | strongest_counter_evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PAT-0001 | The rapid proliferation of GPU-accelerated servers has ended the decade-long era of flat data centre energy use (E02 pdf p. 8, ROW-0026). While global data centre compute instances grew 550% between 2010 and 2018 with only a 6% energy increase to ~205 TWh in 2018 (E01 pdf p. 2, ROW-0010, ROW-0011), US data centre electricity consumption surged from 76 TWh in 2018 to 176 TWh in 2023 (4.4% of total US electricity) and is projected to reach 325 to 580 TWh by 2028 (6.7% to 12.0% of US power) (E02 pdf p. 7-8, ROW-0021, ROW-0023, ROW-0025). Globally, data centre demand is projected to more than double from 415 TWh in 2024 to ~945 TWh by 2030 and ~1,200 TWh by 2035 in the IEA Base Case (E03 pdf p. 14, ROW-0037, ROW-0041, ROW-0042), with 2025 AI accelerator Thermal Design Power (TDP) reaching 6.8 GW for NVIDIA/AMD and up to 12.0 GW total (E04 pdf p. 4, ROW-0061, ROW-0062). | D / B | E01, E02, E03, E04, E05 | None | GRP-01, GRP-02, GRP-07 |  | ROW-0004, ROW-0010, ROW-0011, ROW-0021, ROW-0023, ROW-0025, ROW-0026, ROW-0037, ROW-0041, ROW-0042, ROW-0061, ROW-0062, ROW-0069 | E01 p. 1, 2; E02 p. 7, 8; E03 p. 14; E04 p. 4; E05 p. 1 | Three independent primary lineage groups: GRP-01 (LBNL server stock modeling), GRP-02 (de Vries-Gao chip supply-chain modeling), and GRP-07 (IEA global energy system balances). Note: E01 and E02 share coauthors (GRP-01); E04 and E05 share the same author (GRP-02). | 0 | None. All sources confirm inflection away from flat demand. |
| PAT-0002 | Building new high-voltage transmission lines requires 4 to 8 years in advanced economies (E03 pdf p. 15, ROW-0049), and supply-chain wait times for critical grid components such as transformers and cables doubled between 2021 and 2024 (E03 pdf p. 15, ROW-0050), lagging behind the 1 to 2 year deployment cycle of hyperscale facilities. In Virginia, utilities can protect grid reliability by delaying new large customer additions (E06 pdf p. 9, ROW-0090) as unconstrained growth requires adding 3.6 to 3.8 GW/yr of generation and 8.7 GW of transmission transfer capacity between 2025 and 2040 (E07 pdf p. 17-18, ROW-0105, ROW-0107). In India, grid connection approvals slow deployment (E08 pdf p. 4, ROW-0125) with 2 to 3 GW concentrated on the Kalwa/Padghe corridor into Mumbai by 2030 (E09 pdf p. 14, ROW-0150). | DIS-0001 | Disagreement | Decoupling of Compute vs Decoupling Failure | E01 (Masanet et al. 2020) | Global data centre energy use was estimated at ~205 TWh in 2018 (representing a 6% increase from 2010 despite a 550% increase in compute instances) (pdf p. 2, ROW-0010, ROW-0011). | pdf p. 2, ROW-0010 | E02 (Shehabi et al. 2024) | US data centre electricity consumption surged 131% from 76 TWh in 2018 (1.9% of US power) to 176 TWh in 2023 (4.4% of US power), and is projected to reach 325 to 580 TWh by 2028 (6.7% to 12.0% of US power) (pdf p. 7, 8, ROW-0020, ROW-0021, ROW-0023, ROW-0025). | pdf p. 7, ROW-0021; pdf p. 8, ROW-0023 | Methodological vintage and architectural regime shift [I]. E01 analyzed the virtualization and hyperscale consolidation era (2010-2018). E02 captured the post-2018 explosion of GPU-accelerated computing and generative AI workloads where server wattage increased from ~500W to 10-40 kW per rack. | Cross-checked across E01, E02, E03, E04, E05 | Strong empirical rigor in both; divergence reflects genuine historical transition [I] |
| DIS-0002 | Disagreement | Data Centre Share of National Electricity Consumption | E09 / E11_P (India) | Data centres are projected to represent 2.6% to 3.3% of India's electricity consumption by 2030 (E09 pdf p. 14, ROW-0149), with AI data centres adding 26.3 GW of peak load by FY 2031-32 (~6.78% of 388 GW peak) (E11_P pdf p. 1, 3, ROW-0170, ROW-0174). | pdf p. 14, ROW-0149; pdf p. 1, ROW-0170 | E16 (Ireland) | Data centres consumed 7,663 GWh or 23% of total national metered electricity in 2025, exceeding the 18% share consumed by all urban residential dwellings (E16 pdf p. 1, ROW-0175, ROW-0176, ROW-0178). | pdf p. 1, ROW-0175, ROW-0176 | Economic scale and geographic denominator divergence [I]. Ireland possesses a small national grid (~33 TWh total) hosting massive European hyperscale headquarters. India possesses a continental grid (~1,600+ TWh) with massive agricultural, industrial, and railway baseloads, diluting national percentage shares despite huge absolute gigawatt additions. | Cross-checked across E08, E09, E11_P, E16, E17, E19 | Robust official statistics in both; highlights that national share metrics cannot be directly compared without contextualizing grid denominator [I] |
| DIS-0003 | Disagreement | Interconnection Queue Capacity vs Utility Metered Planning | E15 (Trade Pipeline) | Dominion Energy's contracted data center connection pipeline reached 53.8 GW in July 2026 across its service territory, as reported by a secondary trade source (pdf p. 2, WEB-0022). | pdf p. 2, WEB-0022 | E06 (Legislative Audit) | Dominion plans and builds transmission to serve 1,000 MW of forecasted metered load even when data centre customers request 2,000 MW of capacity (pdf p. 69, ROW-0101). | pdf p. 69, ROW-0101 | Contractual multi-queuing and speculative developer behavior [I]. Developers submit identical multi-hundred-megawatt applications across adjacent substations to secure priority, inflating paper queues 2x-3x above physical transformer delivery capacity. | Cross-checked across E02, E06, E07, E13, E14, E15 | Queue figures are highly speculative; legislative audit and utility metered load data provide much higher empirical validity [I] |
| GAP-0001 | Literature Gap | Absence of Dynamic Load Models for Indian Power Grids | E09 (FSR Global 2026) | GRID-INDIA and SLDCs lack validated dynamic simulation models representing data centre electrical load response during grid faults or frequency sags (pdf p. 10, ROW-0157). | pdf p. 10, ROW-0157 | Checked Sources | E08, E09, E11_P, E12 | E08 p. 14; E11_P p. 1; E12 p. 1 | Indian transmission planning models data centres as static P-Q bus loads rather than dynamic electronic converter loads with UPS fault ride-through thresholds [I]. | E08, E09, E11_P, E12 checked thoroughly; none contain empirical dynamic simulation parameters | High priority research gap for Indian grid stability [I] |
| GAP-0002 | Literature Gap | Corporate Non-Disaggregation of AI vs General Cloud Workloads | E04 / E05 (Academic Literature) | Google stopped disclosing machine learning energy share after 2021 (E04 pdf p. 1, ROW-0057), and operators refuse to disclose facility-level power or water metrics, masking AI consumption (E05 pdf p. 10, ROW-0083). | pdf p. 1, ROW-0057; pdf p. 10, ROW-0083 | Checked Sources | E01, E02, E03, E04, E05 | E01 p. 1; E02 p. 7; E03 p. 14; E04 p. 1; E05 p. 10 | Commercial confidentiality and competitive posturing around proprietary AI model training efficiencies [I]. | E01, E02, E03, E04, E05 checked; all confirm lack of direct corporate disaggregation | Forces researchers to rely on upstream silicon packaging proxies (TSMC CoWoS) [I] |

No comparable cost-of-service studies exist for Indian states [I]. | pdf p. 9, ROW-0091; pdf p. 19, ROW-0109 | Checked Sources | E08, E09, E11_P, E12 | E08 p. 14; E09 p. 24; E11_P p. 1; E12 p. 3 | Absence of published cost-of-service studies by state electricity regulatory commissions (MERC, APERC, TSERC, TNERC) evaluating data centre infrastructure cost allocation [I]. | E08, E09, E11_P, E12 checked; Indian literature discusses discom revenue erosion qualitatively but reports zero econometric bill impact numbers | Critical gap for dissertation research on Indian state equity [I] |

| WEAK-0002 | Weakest Evidence | Corporate Market-Based Renewable Energy Scope-2 Accounting | E05 (de Vries-Gao 2026) | Global data centre electricity implied carbon intensity was 395.65 gCO2/kWh in 2024 (E05 pdf p. 2, ROW-0077), while corporate sustainability reporting allows tech firms to use market-based Scope-2 accounting where Meta claims 98% clean energy through annual PPAs (E05 pdf p. 5, ROW-0081). | pdf p. 2, ROW-0077; pdf p. 5, ROW-0081 | Checked Sources | E03, E05, E08, E10_P1 | E03 p. 14; E05 p. 2; E08 p. 14; E10_P1 p. 4 | Annual volumetric renewable energy matching masks hourly grid carbon intensity and reliance on fossil backup during renewable lulls [I]. | Accounting construct vs physical reality [I]. | WEAK-0003 | Weakest Evidence | Contracted Interconnection Queues vs Operational Metered Demand | Primary E02, E06; Secondary E15 | Primary source E02 notes tracking interconnection queues risks overestimation due to projects applying in multiple utility queues (pdf p. 8, ROW-0029). Primary source E06 notes Dominion plans transmission to serve 1,000 MW metered load when customers request 2,000 MW capacity (pdf p. 69, ROW-0101). Secondary trade press source E15 reports Dominion's contracted queue reached 53.8 GW in July 2026, labelled as reported by a secondary source (pdf p. 2, WEB-0022). | pdf p. 8, ROW-0029; pdf p. 69, ROW-0101; pdf p. 2, WEB-0022 | Checked Sources | E06, E07, E15 | E02 p. 8 (ROW-0029); E06 p. 69 (ROW-0101); E15 p. 2 (WEB-0022) | pdf p. 8, ROW-0029; pdf p. 69, ROW-0101; pdf p. 2, WEB-0022 | Paper queue distortion [I]. Interconnection queue numbers overestimate physical demand due to duplicate applications across utility service areas. Trade press numbers (53.8 GW) reflect speculative paper pipeline rather than committed construction [I]. | Should never be cited as anticipated real electrical demand without severe haircuts [I] |

## 2.7 Methodological Quality Matrix (Energy)
| paper_id | peer_reviewed_or_official | data_sources_named | method_explained | system_boundary_stated | limits_discussed | india_covered_with_own_data | independent_of_industry | reproducible | scoring_rationale_and_pages | Field_12 | Field_13 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| E01 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 1 | 1 | HIGH | Bottom-up server stock model | Older vintage (2010-2018); predates generative AI accelerator explosion |
| E02 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | HIGH | Bottom-up facility & chip model | Forecasting rapid AI adoption entails significant uncertainty; utility filings heavily redacted |
| E03 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | Global macroeconomic energy model | Relies partly on proprietary analyst tracking (SemiAnalysis, IDC) for AI hardware shipments |
| E04 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 1 | 2 | 1 | 1 | MEDIUM | Supply-chain packaging proxy | Theoretical TDP upper bound does not capture real-world operational power throttling |
| E05 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 1 | 1 | 1 | MEDIUM | Triangulated literature synthesis | Indirect carbon/water estimations rely on company-wide averages rather than metered data |
| E06 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | State legislative audit & rate study | Relies on utility load forecasts subject to macroeconomic market shifts |
| E07 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | Production-cost capacity expansion | Assumes fixed-price market capacity purchases up to 3 GW; unproven advanced nuclear reliance |
| E08 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | Empirical think-tank synthesis | Limited facility-level metered transparency; proprietary operator PUE confidentiality |
| E09 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | Power engineering technical review | Lack of validated dynamic simulation models for data centre loads in Indian grid studies |
| E10_P1 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | Statutory regulatory decision | Applies prospectively to new applicants; does not retroactively restructure existing connections |
| E11_P | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | HIGH | Official parliamentary record | National aggregate forecast (26.3 GW) without state-level or nodal disaggregation |
| E16 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | HIGH | Official revenue meter billing census | Reports metered consumption only; does not capture behind-the-meter captive generation |

| indicator_id | my_indicator_label | literature_closest_variable | source_row_id | pdf_page | measured_same_way_I | what_literature_cannot_tell_me | suggested_caveat_I |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IND-MAP-0001 | state peak demand and peak demand not met (CEA, August 2026) | National and state coincident peak electric demand (GW) and loss-of-load probability (pdf p. 1, ROW-0170; pdf p. 3, ROW-0174; E07 p. 16, ROW-0104) | ROW-0104, ROW-0170, ROW-0174 | E11_P p. 1, 3; E07 p. 16 | partly | Literature reports macro all-India peak forecasts (388 GW by FY32) and Virginia peak forecasts (60 GW), but does not report district-level or monthly peak deficits for Indian states [I]. | Peak demand figures mix diverse non-coincident industrial loads; data centre baseload is flat (load factor >0.85) unlike weather-sensitive residential summer peaks [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0002 | energy not supplied (%) | Loss of load probability (LOLP) and grid frequency excursion duration above statutory ceiling (pdf p. 10, ROW-0119; pdf p. 20, ROW-0153, ROW-0154) | ROW-0119, ROW-0153, ROW-0154 | E07 p. 10; E09 p. 20 | no | Literature tracks system-level frequency excursions (50.39 Hz events in Aug 2024) and generation LOLP, but does not provide distribution-level unserved energy by district [I]. | Energy not supplied at DISCOM level reflects distribution transformer failures, whereas data centre grid impacts manifest as transmission thermal overloads and frequency deviations [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0003 | rural and urban hours of power supply (state, FY 2023-24) | Continuous 24/7 power supply requirements (pdf p. 12, ROW-0127) vs residential feeder reliability (pdf p. 1, ROW-0178) | ROW-0127, ROW-0178 | E08 p. 12; E16 p. 1 | no | Sources quantify that data centres require uninterrupted 24/7 power (E08 pdf p. 12, ROW-0127) and extreme spatial load concentration (Ireland DCs exceeding all urban homes, E16 pdf p. 1, ROW-0178), but do not publish rural/urban feeder supply hours [I]. | Feeder supply hours reflect agricultural rationing and DISCOM power procurement scheduling, not physical substation presence of data centres [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0004 | serving DISCOM and its 14th Integrated Rating grade | DISCOM financial stress, fixed-cost socialization, and cross-subsidy surcharge erosion from open access (pdf p. 14, ROW-0131; pdf p. 70, ROW-0102; pdf p. 19, ROW-0110, ROW-0111) | ROW-0102, ROW-0110, ROW-0111, ROW-0131 | E06 p. 70; E07 p. 19; E08 p. 14 | no | Literature analyzes DISCOM commercial revenue erosion from large loads using open access, but does not correlate utility credit ratings with data centre penetration [I]. | A poorly rated DISCOM (e.g. Grade C/D) faces severe borrowing cost penalties when financing dedicated 220 kV substation upgrades demanded by data centre developers [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0005 | transmission company (STU / InSTS) | Inter-State Transmission System (ISTS) and Intra-State Transmission System (InSTS) capacity additions and regional transfer bottlenecks (pdf p. 2, ROW-0171, ROW-0172) | ROW-0171, ROW-0172 | E11_P p. 2 | yes | Literature reports national aggregate InSTS lines under construction (30,387 ckm, 118,194 MVA), but does not break down nodal transformer headroom by STU [I]. | STU boundary defines transmission wheeling charges and intra-state open access regulations, which CEEW (E08) identifies as the primary regulatory friction point [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0006 | grid-constraint evidence by district | Substation-by-substation capacity bottlenecks, transmission corridor saturation, and transformer lead times (pdf p. 15, ROW-0134; pdf p. 14, ROW-0150; pdf p. 1, WEB-0016; pdf p. 3, WEB-0020) | ROW-0134, ROW-0150, WEB-0016, WEB-0020 | E08 p. 15; E09 p. 14; E13 p. 1-2; E14 p. 3 | yes | Sources document specific corridor bottlenecks (Kalwa/Padghe in Mumbai, Eastern Loudoun in Virginia), but lack comprehensive district-level grid constraint registers across all Indian states [I]. | Grid constraints are hyper-local: a district may have surplus generation 100 km away but face local 220/33 kV transformation deficits [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0007 | live and planned data centre MW per district (IT load and facility load kept separate) | Operational MW vs pipeline MW, rack power density (kW/rack), and IT load vs facility power via PUE (pdf p. 2, ROW-0059; pdf p. 45, ROW-0096, ROW-0097; pdf p. 12, ROW-0146; pdf p. 2, WEB-0021) | ROW-0059, ROW-0096, ROW-0097, ROW-0146, WEB-0021 | E04 p. 2; E06 p. 45; E09 p. 12; E15 p. 2 | yes | Literature provides city-level operational totals (Mumbai 53%, 1,700 MW India total), but does not break down live vs planned MW by administrative district [I]. | Crucial to enforce PUE multiplier: an IT load of 100 MW translates to 120-140 MW facility grid demand depending on cooling technology [I]. |
| IND-MAP-0008 | planned MW as a share of state peak (ranking only, because it mixes stages) | Data centre peak demand as a share of utility / regional coincident peak (pdf p. 6, ROW-0085; pdf p. 23, ROW-0114; pdf p. 1, ROW-0170) | ROW-0085, ROW-0114, ROW-0170 | E06 p. 6; E07 p. 23; E11_P p. 1 | yes | Literature calculates utility peak shares (Dominion 25% peak, Ireland 23% metered), but warns against mixing speculative queue requests with operational load [I]. | Rankings are valid for identifying state-level grid exposure, but summing announced pipeline MW directly overstates immediate grid impact due to 50%+ drop-out rates [I]. |

## 2.12 Methodological Decisions Log (Energy)
| decision_id | context_or_source | issue_unclear | chosen_conservative_option | reasoning_and_rule_basis |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D01 | Source Selection (E01-E04, E10-E11) | Multiple document versions existed (preprints, landing pages, news summaries vs official PDFs) | Selected publisher-original authoritative PDFs with printed page numbers where available; retained primary government/regulatory decisions over press. | Hard Rule 1 & Source Type Rules: Prioritize original publisher PDFs; ensure page locators and verbatim text are permanently reproducible. |
| D02 | Web & Press Classification (E10_S1/S2, E11_S, E12, E13-E19) | Distinction between independent peer-reviewed/official evidence and secondary media reporting | Strictly designated all web, trade press, and consultant summaries as SECONDARY; barred them from counting as independent support in Patterns. | Source Type Rules: Web/press sources are secondary and must not inflate independent consensus counts. |
| D03 | Text Normalization in Automated Verifier | Soft hyphens (\xad), discretionary line breaks, and curly quotes caused false verification failures on verbatim text | Built rigorous normalization pipeline: soft hyphens at line breaks (\xad\n) joined into full words, typographic quotes/dashes normalized, numeric hyphens preserved. | Page & Quote Rules: Automated verification requires exact character matches while properly resolving typesetting artifacts without altering semantics. |
| D04 | System Boundary Separation (IT Load vs Facility Load vs Contracted MVA) | Literature reports data centre energy across different electrical boundaries (IT server TDP vs total facility power vs contracted substation capacity) | Enforced strict boundary labeling in Extraction (Unit and Variable_Name) and barred direct merger in Normalised_Values without explicit formulas. | Variable Category A & Normalised_Values Rules: Never merge different system boundaries without clear mathematical and conceptual disaggregation. |
| D05 | Printed Page Numbers for Web-Saved PDFs | Web-derived and press PDFs lack formal journal printed page numbers | Recorded printed_page as 'locator in my file, not a page of the source' and flagged page_flag as 'PDF page only', recording section and paragraph. | Source Type Rules: Avoid conflating PDF file position with source pagination for HTML/press prints. |
| D06 | Quarantine of Inferred Content | Distinguishing source-stated factual data from researcher synthesis and analytical evaluation | Tagged all factual extractions strictly with [S]; quarantined all methodological inferences, cross-source conversions, and evaluations under [I] tags with visual color fill. | Hard Rule 3 & Page Rules: Interpretation goes only in columns marked [I]; zero subjective distortion in primary extraction. |
| D07 | Exclusion of Water Footprint Data from E05 and E08 | E05 and E08 contain both water consumption data and energy/grid data | Restricted extractions for this workbook strictly to energy demand, electricity consumption, and electrical grid integration findings; excluded all water-only rows. | Stage 2 Rule: Extract energy and grid findings only; avoid duplication of water dissertation rows. |

### 3.5 Flagged Items Quality Audit
1. **Over-25-Word Quotes:** **0 flagged**. An automated word-count scan across all 452 extraction rows confirmed that every quote is within the 25-word cap. (Max quote length observed was 25 words, enforced during the staged workbook build).
2. **Missing Page Numbers:** **0 flagged**. All 452 extraction records carry explicit page locators (PDF page, printed page number, or paragraph number for web-print files).
3. **Unverified Quotes:** **0 flagged**. All quotes in the primary Extraction sheets were mechanically verified against primary PDF texts via automated string matching during workbook compilation (recorded with PASS status in Checks sheets).
4. **Empty Required Fields:** **0 flagged**. All empty cells in original spreadsheets were explicitly converted to 'NOT RECORDED', 'None', or 'NONE STATED' to prevent silent data gaps.
5. **Lineage Identification:** **100% accounted for**. In `lit_energy_extractions.md`, every single row carries its formal lineage group ID (GRP-01 to GRP-10) and lineage name. In `lit_water_extractions.md`, shared dataset citations (`cited_from_other_source`) are explicitly recorded per row.
